[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, G31.84, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps... it just goes blank, and it’s been scaring me. Like at work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. The same steps I’ve done for years.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because people expect you to know what you’re doing out there, and traffic doesn’t really give you time to stand there thinking. I used to be good at routines—well, I mean, I still know them, I think—but then my brain just sort of shuts off at the worst time.\n\nDoctor: How long do these blank moments typically last?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not very long, I don’t think… but it feels long when I’m standing there with the paddle in my hand. Like I’ll look at the road-closure forms or the stop-slow sign and suddenly the steps are just gone, even though I’ve done them for years.\n\nSometimes it’s just a few seconds, maybe longer if I start panicking about it. It’s hard to tell, because when your mind goes blank out there, time gets weird.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments seem to happen more often when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think stress makes it worse, yeah… or maybe I notice it more when I’m stressed. Like when I’m standing there with the stop-slow paddle and cars are waiting, people are looking, and then I glance at the road-closure forms and my mind just goes blank. I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years.\n\nIt’s strange because it’s not like I don’t know my job. I used to keep everything in order, all the little routines, and then suddenly it’s like someone pulled the plug. And then I start thinking about how dangerous it is to freeze there, and that makes me more anxious, and then it’s harder to get the steps back.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, but work is where I notice it most because there are forms and cars and people waiting. At home it’s more like I’ll walk into a room and just stand there, and then I start thinking about the road-closure forms again, because that’s when it really scares me.\n\nIt’s like my mind goes blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking right at the stop-slow paddle or the paperwork, and suddenly I can’t remember steps I’ve done for years. And then I wonder, if I can blank on that, what else am I blanking on?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things like appointments, birthdays, or tasks you need to do at home?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so, but the thing I came in for is more that my mind just blanks out at the worst times. Like I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years.\n\nAt home it’s harder to explain, because appointments and things… I try to write them down, but then I start worrying about work again, because that’s where it feels dangerous. Like if I forget a birthday, that’s awful, but if I freeze with cars waiting, that’s different, you know? It just… it makes me feel like my brain isn’t reliable.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or fatigue when your mind goes blank?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed a headache, like not in a clear way where I can say, “Oh, that happens every time.” I do get worn out from worrying about it, but when it happens I’m mostly focused on the blankness itself.\n\nLike I’ll be standing there and then afterward I’m thinking, did I look normal, did anyone see me freeze, did I mess up the road setup… and then I forget to even pay attention to my body. So I’m not sure. Maybe tired, but I can’t say for sure it’s connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty recalling memories from your past, such as childhood events or important life milestones?\n\nPatient: Um… from my past? I don’t know. I mean, I remember some things, but not like… not in a neat order. Childhood is kind of patchy, but I always thought everyone’s was, you know?\n\nImportant milestones… I guess I can remember the big labels of things, but if you asked me details, I might get mixed up. Like I’ll start trying to think about one memory and then I end up thinking about whether I left something written down at home, or the forms at work, and then I lose the thread.\n\nSo I’m not sure if it’s really memory from the past, or just me not being able to stay with the question long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these blank moments are affecting your overall quality of life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I keep thinking of it as a work problem because that’s where it started feeling obvious, like I stopped being someone people could count on. I couldn’t finish the road-closure paperwork the way I used to, and then I’d be second-guessing the traffic-safety steps, like, did I do that already, am I about to do something wrong… and when your job is around cars, that’s not just embarrassing.\n\nBut outside work, yes. I pulled back from things. I used to do choir, and volunteering, and I’d see family more, but then it got easier to just not go, because I didn’t want people asking what was wrong or seeing me get stuck. And then I guess it got bad enough that my family felt like they had to bring me in for help. I didn’t really want it to be like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with friends and family since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t reach out like I used to. I used to be the person who’d organize things, like choir rides, or those garden swap things, and making sure snacks were covered for youth basketball… I had lists, and I’d call people, and it felt normal.\n\nNow I can just sit there with my phone in my hand for hours, seeing messages, and I don’t answer. It’s not even always that I’m upset with anybody. I just don’t feel that push to do it, like the part of me that used to jump up and handle things is… not there.\n\nAnd then time passes, and it feels awkward, so I wait longer. So yes, with friends and family, I’m more quiet. I don’t mean to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these memory problems are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is. I mean, I keep thinking maybe if I had a better notebook or if I put things in different colored folders—because I used to be really good with that, actually, like for choir I had this whole system with rides and snacks and who needed what—but at work it stopped mattering how many notes I made.\n\nI couldn’t finish the road-closure paperwork the way I was supposed to, and then I’d get scared because I couldn’t trust myself with the traffic-safety steps. Like, did I already check that, did I miss something, am I going to cause a problem? And that made me not reliable, which… that’s hard to say.\n\nAnd with people, I just pulled back. Choir, volunteering, family things—I stopped showing up. Not all at once, it was more like one missed thing and then another. And then it got to the point where my family felt like they had to bring me in for help. So… yes, it’s affecting both.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that you would normally remember, even when you're not feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. It’s not only when I’m upset or rushed. That’s what scares me a little, because I’ll just be standing there looking at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and my mind just… goes blank.\n\nAnd these are steps I’ve done for years. It’s not new. It’s not like when my niece tried to show me how to use some app and I couldn’t find the button—this is my job, things I should know. I’ll think, wait, did I already do this part? What comes next? And then I get stuck staring at it like the answer is supposed to appear if I look long enough. So yes, I forget things I normally would remember, even when nothing obvious is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from before these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s memories from before, exactly… like, I can remember plenty of things, I think. I remember old choir stuff, and how we used to arrange rides, and the garden swap tables—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIt’s more like… I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. Things that should be automatic just disappear for a moment.\n\nSo it’s not like my whole past is gone. It’s more that the thinking part just drops out when I need it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're often confused, even after the blank moments have passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sort of. I mean, after it passes I can usually move again, but I’m still confused about what just happened, or what step I was on.\n\nLike with the road-closure forms, I’ll be looking at them and then it’s like everything empties out, and afterward I’m thinking, okay, was I halfway through this part or did I not start it? Same with the stop-slow paddle—something I’ve done for years, and suddenly I can’t remember the order of things.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to organize choir papers, because I always knew where everything went, and now even simple steps feel like… I don’t know, like someone shuffled the pages in my head. So yes, I guess the confusion doesn’t always just disappear right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disoriented after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think disoriented is fair, maybe not like I don’t know where I am, but like I’ve lost my place in everything.\n\nIt happens at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and then suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. Afterward I’m standing there trying to piece it back together, like, what was I doing, what comes next?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because those are things I should just know. I used to be good at keeping procedures straight—well, with work things, and even with choir schedules before, all those papers and sign-up lists—but now sometimes it’s like my mind just drops the thread. So yes, afterward I feel… thrown off. Disoriented, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it takes a long time to feel normal again after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can. I mean, sometimes the blank part is only a moment, but feeling right again takes longer, because I’m still trying to remember what I was doing.\n\nLike I said, I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. And then even after my mind comes back, I don’t just snap back to normal. I’m sort of standing there, trying to rebuild the order in my head.\n\nIt’s like when you lose your place in a song—sorry, choir again—but everyone else keeps going and you’re trying to find the measure. That’s what it feels like. So yes, it takes me a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things or picking up new skills since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s hard to tell what counts as “new,” because lately even the old things feel new in the worst way.\n\nLike I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. So if I can’t hold onto those steps, picking up anything new feels… I don’t know, like trying to write on a wet piece of paper.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how I used to manage things fine, like organizing papers and schedules—choir stuff, work stuff, all of that—and now I get stuck on things I should already know. So yes, learning new skills has been harder, but mostly because my mind blanks out right when I need it to stay with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to find the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, sometimes it’s words, but more than that it’s like the whole thought just disappears before I can say it.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. And then if someone asks me something right then, I just sort of… stare, because I can’t find what I’m supposed to say or do next.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know the words ever, exactly. It’s like they’re behind a door for a minute. Then I get embarrassed and try to cover it, which probably makes me talk around it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any strong emotions, like sadness or anxiety, after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sadness, mostly. Anxiety too, I guess, because I’m worried it’ll happen again, but the sadness is the heavier part.\n\nHonestly, most mornings I already wake up with this hollow, heavy feeling in my chest, like the day’s ruined before I even get my feet on the floor. And then if I have one of those blank moments, it kind of confirms it, you know? Like, “See, you’re not okay today either.”\n\nIt reminds me of when you miss your entrance in choir and everyone else is still singing—sorry, I keep going back to that—but afterward you don’t just feel confused, you feel… ashamed and sad. Like something’s wrong with you. So yes, after it passes, I still feel pretty low.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F43.8", "G31.84", "R41.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

---------- turn 19 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========

I think so… yes. It feels like it’s getting harder to trust my own mind.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my mind keeps... it just goes blank, and it’s been scaring me. Like at work, I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. The same steps I’ve done for years.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because people expect you to know what you’re doing out there, and traffic doesn’t really give you time to stand there thinking. I used to be good at routines—well, I mean, I still know them, I think—but then my brain just sort of shuts off at the worst time.\n\nDoctor: How long do these blank moments typically last?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s not very long, I don’t think… but it feels long when I’m standing there with the paddle in my hand. Like when I’m standing there with the stop-slow paddle and cars are waiting, people are looking, and then I glance at the road-closure forms and my mind just goes blank. I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years.\n\nIt’s strange because it’s not like I don’t know my job. I used to keep everything in order, all the little routines, and then suddenly it’s like someone pulled the plug. Like I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years.\n\nAt home it’s harder to explain, because appointments and things… I try to write them down, but then I start worrying about work again, because that’s where it feels dangerous. Like if I forget a birthday, that’s awful, but if I freeze with cars waiting, that’s different, you know? It just… it makes me feel like my brain isn’t reliable.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like headaches or fatigue when your mind goes blank?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed a headache, like not in a clear way where I can say, “Oh, that happens every time.” I do get worn out from worrying about it, but when it happens I’m mostly focused on the blankness itself.\n\nLike I’ll be standing there and then afterward I’m thinking, did I look normal, did anyone see me freeze, did I mess up the road setup… and then I forget to even pay attention to my body. So I’m not sure. Maybe tired, but I can’t say for sure it’s connected.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty recalling memories from your past, such as childhood events or important life milestones?\n\nPatient: Um… from my past? I don’t know. I mean, I remember some things, but not like… not in a neat order. Childhood is kind of patchy, but I always thought everyone’s was, you know?\n\nImportant milestones… I guess I can remember the big labels of things, but if you asked me details, I might get mixed up. Like I’ll start trying to think about one memory and then I end up thinking about whether I left something written down at home, or the forms at work, and then I lose the thread.\n\nSo I’m not sure if it’s really memory from the past, or just me not being able to stay with the question long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these blank moments are affecting your overall quality of life or relationships outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, I keep thinking of it as a work problem because that’s where it started feeling obvious, like I stopped being someone people could count on. I couldn’t finish the road-closure paperwork the way I used to, and then I’d be second-guessing the traffic-safety steps, like, did I do that already, am I about to do something wrong… and when your job is around cars, that’s not just embarrassing.\n\nBut outside work, yes. I pulled back from things. I used to do choir, and volunteering, and I’d see family more, but then it got easier to just not go, because I didn’t want people asking what was wrong or seeing me get stuck. And then I guess it got bad enough that my family felt like they had to bring me in for help. I didn’t really want it to be like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with friends and family since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t reach out like I used to. I used to be the person who’d organize things, like choir rides, or those garden swap things, and making sure snacks were covered for youth basketball… I had lists, and I’d call people, and it felt normal.\n\nNow I can just sit there with my phone in my hand for hours, seeing messages, and I don’t answer. It’s not even always that I’m upset with anybody. I just don’t feel that push to do it, like the part of me that used to jump up and handle things is… not there.\n\nAnd then time passes, and it feels awkward, so I wait longer. So yes, with friends and family, I’m more quiet. I don’t mean to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these memory problems are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is. I mean, I keep thinking maybe if I had a better notebook or if I put things in different colored folders—because I used to be really good with that, actually, like for choir I had this whole system with rides and snacks and who needed what—but at work it stopped mattering how many notes I made.\n\nI couldn’t finish the road-closure paperwork the way I was supposed to, and then I’d get scared because I couldn’t trust myself with the traffic-safety steps. Like, did I already check that, did I miss something, am I going to cause a problem? And that made me not reliable, which… that’s hard to say.\n\nAnd with people, I just pulled back. Choir, volunteering, family things—I stopped showing up. Not all at once, it was more like one missed thing and then another. And then it got to the point where my family felt like they had to bring me in for help. So… yes, it’s affecting both.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting things that you would normally remember, even when you're not feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. It’s not only when I’m upset or rushed. That’s what scares me a little, because I’ll just be standing there looking at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and my mind just… goes blank.\n\nAnd these are steps I’ve done for years. It’s not new. It’s not like when my niece tried to show me how to use some app and I couldn’t find the button—this is my job, things I should know. I’ll think, wait, did I already do this part? What comes next? And then I get stuck staring at it like the answer is supposed to appear if I look long enough. So yes, I forget things I normally would remember, even when nothing obvious is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from before these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s memories from before, exactly… like, I can remember plenty of things, I think. I remember old choir stuff, and how we used to arrange rides, and the garden swap tables—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIt’s more like… I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. Things that should be automatic just disappear for a moment.\n\nSo it’s not like my whole past is gone. It’s more that the thinking part just drops out when I need it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're often confused, even after the blank moments have passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sort of. I mean, after it passes I can usually move again, but I’m still confused about what just happened, or what step I was on.\n\nLike with the road-closure forms, I’ll be looking at them and then it’s like everything empties out, and afterward I’m thinking, okay, was I halfway through this part or did I not start it? Same with the stop-slow paddle—something I’ve done for years, and suddenly I can’t remember the order of things.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to organize choir papers, because I always knew where everything went, and now even simple steps feel like… I don’t know, like someone shuffled the pages in my head. So yes, I guess the confusion doesn’t always just disappear right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disoriented after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think disoriented is fair, maybe not like I don’t know where I am, but like I’ve lost my place in everything.\n\nIt happens at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and then suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. Afterward I’m standing there trying to piece it back together, like, what was I doing, what comes next?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because those are things I should just know. I used to be good at keeping procedures straight—well, with work things, and even with choir schedules before, all those papers and sign-up lists—but now sometimes it’s like my mind just drops the thread. So yes, afterward I feel… thrown off. Disoriented, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it takes a long time to feel normal again after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can. I mean, sometimes the blank part is only a moment, but feeling right again takes longer, because I’m still trying to remember what I was doing.\n\nLike I said, I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. And then even after my mind comes back, I don’t just snap back to normal. I’m sort of standing there, trying to rebuild the order in my head.\n\nIt’s like when you lose your place in a song—sorry, choir again—but everyone else keeps going and you’re trying to find the measure. That’s what it feels like. So yes, it takes me a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things or picking up new skills since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. But yes, it does feel like it’s happening more, or at least it scares me more because it’s with things I should know.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these blank moments are happening more frequently now than when they first started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 41 / crossing_guard_or_flagger / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times, like I will look at the road-closure forms or the stop-slow paddle and suddenly cannot remember the steps I have done for years.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is more frequent now. Or maybe it feels that way because it’s happening when I really need my head to work.

Like, I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s strange because those things should be automatic by now.

It’s like—sorry, this is probably not answering exactly—but when you do something over and over, like a song you know by heart, and then suddenly there’s just a hole where the next part should be. That’s what it feels like. And yes, it seems to be happening more than when it first started.

It’s not new. It’s not like when my niece tried to show me how to use some app and I couldn’t find the button—this is my job, things I should know. I’ll think, wait, did I already do this part? What comes next? And then I get stuck staring at it like the answer is supposed to appear if I look long enough. So yes, I forget things I normally would remember, even when nothing obvious is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling memories from before these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s memories from before, exactly… like, I can remember plenty of things, I think. I remember old choir stuff, and how we used to arrange rides, and the garden swap tables—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nIt’s more like… I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. Things that should be automatic just disappear for a moment.\n\nSo it’s not like my whole past is gone. It’s more that the thinking part just drops out when I need it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're often confused, even after the blank moments have passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sort of. I mean, after it passes I can usually move again, but I’m still confused about what just happened, or what step I was on.\n\nLike with the road-closure forms, I’ll be looking at them and then it’s like everything empties out, and afterward I’m thinking, okay, was I halfway through this part or did I not start it? Same with the stop-slow paddle—something I’ve done for years, and suddenly I can’t remember the order of things.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to organize choir papers, because I always knew where everything went, and now even simple steps feel like… I don’t know, like someone shuffled the pages in my head. So yes, I guess the confusion doesn’t always just disappear right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel disoriented after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think disoriented is fair, maybe not like I don’t know where I am, but like I’ve lost my place in everything.\n\nIt happens at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and then suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. Afterward I’m standing there trying to piece it back together, like, what was I doing, what comes next?\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because those are things I should just know. I used to be good at keeping procedures straight—well, with work things, and even with choir schedules before, all those papers and sign-up lists—but now sometimes it’s like my mind just drops the thread. So yes, afterward I feel… thrown off. Disoriented, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it takes a long time to feel normal again after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… it can. I mean, sometimes the blank part is only a moment, but feeling right again takes longer, because I’m still trying to remember what I was doing.\n\nLike I said, I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. And then even after my mind comes back, I don’t just snap back to normal. I’m sort of standing there, trying to rebuild the order in my head.\n\nIt’s like when you lose your place in a song—sorry, choir again—but everyone else keeps going and you’re trying to find the measure. That’s what it feels like. So yes, it takes me a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble learning new things or picking up new skills since these blank moments started happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s hard to tell what counts as “new,” because lately even the old things feel new in the worst way.\n\nLike I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. So if I can’t hold onto those steps, picking up anything new feels… I don’t know, like trying to write on a wet piece of paper.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how I used to manage things fine, like organizing papers and schedules—choir stuff, work stuff, all of that—and now I get stuck on things I should already know. So yes, learning new skills has been harder, but mostly because my mind blanks out right when I need it to stay with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to find the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, sometimes it’s words, but more than that it’s like the whole thought just disappears before I can say it.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. And then if someone asks me something right then, I just sort of… stare, because I can’t find what I’m supposed to say or do next.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know the words ever, exactly. It’s like they’re behind a door for a minute. Then I get embarrassed and try to cover it, which probably makes me talk around it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any strong emotions, like sadness or anxiety, after one of these blank moments has passed?\n\nPatient: Yes… sadness, mostly. Anxiety too, I guess, because I’m worried it’ll happen again, but the sadness is the heavier part.\n\nHonestly, most mornings I already wake up with this hollow, heavy feeling in my chest, like the day’s ruined before I even get my feet on the floor. And then if I have one of those blank moments, it kind of confirms it, you know? Like, “See, you’re not okay today either.”\n\nIt reminds me of when you miss your entrance in choir and everyone else is still singing—sorry, I keep going back to that—but afterward you don’t just feel confused, you feel… ashamed and sad. Like something’s wrong with you. So yes, after it passes, I still feel pretty low.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your blank moments and memory problems are getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I think so… yes. It feels like it’s getting harder to trust my own mind.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. My mind keeps going blank at the worst times—like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. And those aren’t new things, you know? I’ve done them over and over.\n\nIt’s like when you repeat a song for choir and then one day the words just… aren’t there. Sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, it does feel like it’s happening more, or at least it scares me more because it’s with things I should know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these blank moments are happening more frequently now than when they first started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is more frequent now. Or maybe it feels that way because it’s happening when I really need my head to work.\n\nLike, I came in because my mind keeps going blank at the worst times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s strange because those things should be automatic by now.\n\nIt’s like—sorry, this is probably not answering exactly—but when you do something over and over, like a song you know by heart, and then suddenly there’s just a hole where the next part should be. That’s what it feels like. And yes, it seems to be happening more than when it first started."
